Board Investor Memo Drafts a structured board or investor update memo covering progress, key metrics, risks, and specific asks. Use when the user needs to write a board update, investor update, or periodic (monthly/quarterly) stakeholder memo. ⇩ .zip

Board / Investor Memo

When to use this

The user needs to write a periodic update to people with oversight or financial stake in the business (a board, investors, or key partners) — distinct from an internal team report (weekly-ops-report) or a client-facing summary of analysis (executive-summary-writer).

When NOT to use this

  • The audience is internal team members, not board/investor-level stakeholders — use weekly-ops-report.
  • There's no real update since the last one — say that plainly rather than padding a memo with filler.

Workflow

  1. Confirm the reporting period and cadence (monthly, quarterly, ad-hoc) — this sets how much ground the memo needs to cover.
  2. Lead with a short headline summary — 2-3 sentences on the overall state of the business this period, before any detail.
  3. Report the key metrics that actually matter to this business, not a generic list — for a charter business, likely bookings, revenue, occupancy/utilization, and customer satisfaction; for other businesses, adapt. Show period-over-period comparison, not just the current number.
  4. Cover progress against previously stated goals or asks. If a previous memo said "we will do X by this quarter," report on it explicitly — don't let commitments quietly disappear.
  5. State risks and challenges honestly, not just wins. A memo that only reports good news loses credibility and hides problems until they're bigger. Pair each risk with what's being done about it.
  6. End with specific asks, if any — what does the board/investor need to decide, approve, or help with? If there's no ask, say "no action needed this period" explicitly rather than leaving it ambiguous.

Output format

## [Business name] — [Period] Update

Headline: [2-3 sentence overall state]

### Key metrics | Metric | This period | Last period | Change | |---|---|---|---| | ... | ... | ... | ... |

### Progress on prior commitments - [Prior commitment] — [status: done / on track / delayed, and why]

### Risks & challenges - [Risk] — [what's being done about it]

### Asks - [Specific ask, or "no action needed this period"] ```

Common pitfalls

  • Only reporting good news — erodes trust once a real problem surfaces without warning.
  • Vague metrics without period-over-period comparison, making it impossible to tell if things are improving or not.
  • Letting previous commitments go unmentioned instead of explicitly tracking them.
  • A vague or missing ask, leaving the reader unsure if they need to do anything.

Quality checklist

  • Headline states the overall picture in 2-3 sentences
  • Metrics show period-over-period comparison, not just current state
  • Prior commitments are explicitly tracked, not dropped
  • Risks are included, each with a stated mitigation
  • Ends with a clear, specific ask (or explicit "none")

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Metric menus by business type

Choose metrics that actually matter to the specific business — a generic metric list is a sign the memo wasn't tailored.

## Charter / tourism / hospitality Bookings (count and revenue), occupancy/utilization rate, average booking value, cancellation rate, review rating and volume, repeat-customer rate, lead time (booking to trip date).

## Professional services / consulting Active engagements, revenue (billed and pipeline), utilization rate (billable hours), client retention/renewal rate, new business pipeline value.

## General SaaS/subscription (for reference if relevant to a client) MRR/ARR, churn rate, net revenue retention, customer acquisition cost, active user count.

Tone notes for risk sections

Investors and boards generally respond better to a risk stated plainly with a mitigation plan than to a risk buried in cautious language or omitted. Compare:

Weak: "There were some challenges with staffing this quarter that we are monitoring."

Better: "Inquiry response time slowed after a team member's departure in August; we're cross-training a second person on inquiry handling, targeting response time back under 2 hours by [date]."

The second version names the actual problem, its cause, and a concrete fix with a timeline — that's what makes a risk section useful rather than just an acknowledgment.

Proposal SOW Drafter Drafts a client proposal or statement of work, covering scope, deliverables, timeline, fees, and assumptions. Use when the user needs to write a consulting proposal, scope an engagement, draft a SOW, or respond to a client request for a project quote. ⇩ .zip

Proposal / Statement of Work Drafter

When to use this

The user needs a written proposal or SOW to send to a prospective or existing client, defining what work will be done, for how much, and under what terms.

When NOT to use this

  • The engagement scope isn't actually clear yet — clarify scope with the user (or note open questions explicitly in the draft) rather than inventing scope details that weren't discussed.
  • This is a legal contract requiring specific jurisdiction/liability language — draft the business content, but flag that legal review is needed before it's a binding contract, not a substitute for one.

Workflow

  1. Confirm what's actually known vs. assumed before drafting: client name, problem being solved, deliverables discussed, timeline constraints, budget range (if known). Flag anything not yet confirmed rather than filling gaps with invented specifics.
  2. State the problem/objective first, in the client's terms — what they're trying to achieve, not a list of activities. This frames everything that follows.
  3. Define scope explicitly, including what's out of scope. Scope creep is the most common source of engagement friction — an explicit "not included" list prevents later disputes as much as the "included" list does.
  4. List deliverables as concrete artifacts, not vague activities — "a written market-entry recommendation with supporting analysis," not "strategic advisory support."
  5. Lay out a timeline with milestones, not just a single end date — makes progress checkable and sets expectations for when the client will see something.
  6. State fees and payment terms clearly, including what happens if scope changes (a change-order or re-scoping clause) — this protects both sides and should be stated up front, not discovered mid-engagement.
  7. List assumptions and dependencies — anything the engagement depends on the client providing (data access, stakeholder availability, timely feedback) that, if missing, would affect timeline or outcome.
  8. Flag that legal/contractual review is a separate step if this proposal will become a binding agreement — this skill drafts the business content, not a vetted legal contract.

Output format

## Proposal: [Engagement name] — [Client name]

### Objective [What the client is trying to achieve, in their terms]

### Scope Included: [...] Not included: [...]

### Deliverables 1. [Concrete deliverable] 2. ...

### Timeline | Milestone | Target date | |---|---| | ... | ... |

### Fees [Fee structure, payment terms, change-order handling]

### Assumptions & dependencies - [What the engagement assumes/depends on from the client]

### Open questions / to confirm before sending [Anything not yet confirmed with the client] ```

Common pitfalls

  • Vague deliverables that can't be checked off as done or not done.
  • No "not included" list, leaving scope open to drift.
  • No change-order/re-scoping language, making later scope changes awkward to charge for.
  • Inventing specifics (numbers, dates, names) that weren't actually discussed, instead of flagging them as open questions.

Quality checklist

  • Objective is stated in the client's terms, not just a task list
  • Scope includes both "included" and "not included"
  • Deliverables are concrete, checkable artifacts
  • Timeline has milestones, not just an end date
  • Fees, payment terms, and change-order handling are stated
  • Anything unconfirmed is flagged, not invented

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Common fee structures, and when each fits

  • Fixed fee — best when scope is well-defined and deliverables are concrete. Lowest client risk, highest provider risk if scope creeps (which is exactly why the "not included" list matters most here).
  • Time & materials (hourly/daily rate) — best when scope is exploratory or likely to evolve. Lower provider risk, requires trust and transparent time tracking to avoid client concern about runaway hours.
  • Retainer — best for ongoing advisory relationships rather than a single project with an end date; usually paired with a defined monthly scope of availability/hours rather than specific deliverables.
  • Value-based / success fee — tied to an outcome (e.g. % of cost savings identified). Higher potential upside, but requires a very clearly measurable outcome and trust in how it's measured — riskiest to set up well, and usually needs the most explicit definition of how success is measured and attributed.

Change-order clause, example language

"Any request that materially changes scope, deliverables, or timeline from what's described above will be scoped and quoted separately before work begins on it. Minor clarifications within the original scope are included at no extra charge."

This kind of clause protects the relationship as much as the fee — it gives a neutral, pre-agreed process for scope changes instead of an awkward renegotiation once something unexpected comes up mid-engagement.

Assumptions/dependencies — common ones worth stating explicitly

  • Timely access to requested data or documents
  • Availability of key stakeholders for interviews/decisions within the proposed timeline
  • Feedback turnaround time on draft deliverables (state an expected window, e.g. "5 business days")
  • Any third-party tools/systems access needed to complete the work

Government Tender Response Drafts a response to a government RFQ or RFT (Request for Quote/Tender) — understanding of requirements, value proposition, capability and experience, commercial offer, and standard compliance declarations — following the structure procurement panels actually evaluate against. Use when the user needs to respond to a government tender, RFQ, or public-sector procurement request. ⇩ .zip

Government Tender / RFQ Response

When to use this

A government agency or public-sector body has issued an RFQ/RFT and the user needs a structured response — distinct from a general client proposal (proposal-sow-drafter) because government responses are evaluated against explicit, published criteria and require standard compliance declarations.

When NOT to use this

  • A general private-sector client proposal with no formal evaluation criteria or compliance requirements — use proposal-sow-drafter, which is less rigid and more relationship-driven.
  • The RFQ/RFT document and its evaluation criteria haven't actually been reviewed yet — read those first; this response should be built around them, not written generically and hoped to fit.

Workflow

  1. Read the RFQ's evaluation criteria first, before drafting anything — evaluators score against stated criteria, not against how good the response reads. Structure the whole response so each criterion is explicitly, visibly addressed (not just implicitly covered somewhere in the prose).
  2. State understanding of requirements in the buyer's own language, referencing their actual stated objectives and context — a response that reads as generic/templated is an easy one for an evaluator to discount.
  3. Build a genuine value proposition tied to evidence — track record, price competitiveness, specific sector experience relevant to this buyer — not generic claims ("we're the best," "outstanding value") with nothing backing them.
  4. Present the proposed approach concretely — what will actually be done, in what sequence, producing what outputs — mirroring how the RFQ describes the need, so the evaluator can map response to requirement easily.
  5. State the commercial offer clearly — rates, total cost estimate, any panel arrangement or existing pre-negotiated rate that applies.
  6. Address standard declarations explicitly — conflict of interest (or lack thereof), compliance with any stated requirements — missing these can disqualify an otherwise strong response outright, so treat them as non-negotiable inclusions, not an afterthought.

Output format

## Response: [RFQ/RFT name] — [issuing agency]

### Understanding of requirements [In the buyer's language, referencing their stated context]

### Value proposition [Specific, evidence-backed — not generic claims]

### Proposed approach [Concrete steps/phases, mapped to what the RFQ actually asked for]

### Capability & experience [Relevant track record — specific, not a generic capability statement]

### Commercial offer [Rates / total cost / panel arrangement if relevant]

### Declarations - Conflict of interest: [statement] - [Any other standard compliance declarations required by this RFQ]

### Evaluation criteria cross-check [Confirm each stated evaluation criterion is addressed somewhere above — list criterion → where it's addressed] ```

Common pitfalls

  • Writing a strong general proposal that doesn't map explicitly to the RFQ's actual evaluation criteria — loses marks even when the content is good, because evaluators score against criteria, not impressions.
  • Generic value propositions with no specific evidence attached.
  • Missing a required declaration (conflict of interest, compliance statement) — can disqualify the response regardless of quality elsewhere.
  • Templated language that doesn't reference the buyer's specific context, reading as copy-pasted from a previous response.

Quality checklist

  • Every stated evaluation criterion is explicitly addressed and cross-checked at the end
  • Value proposition claims are backed by specific evidence
  • Standard declarations are included, not omitted
  • Language references the buyer's actual context, not generic boilerplate

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: illustrative structure only

This is a generic, illustrative structure — not based on any real client's actual tender content, which stays confidential to that engagement.

Say a fictional consultancy is responding to an RFQ from a public-sector agency for "strategic planning workshop facilitation" support, with published evaluation criteria: (1) understanding of requirements — 20%, (2) relevant experience — 30%, (3) proposed approach — 30%, (4) value for money — 20%.

### Evaluation criteria cross-check (built early, drives the whole draft) | Criterion | Weight | Addressed in | |---|---|---| | Understanding of requirements | 20% | "Understanding of requirements" section | | Relevant experience | 30% | "Capability & experience" section | | Proposed approach | 30% | "Proposed approach" section | | Value for money | 20% | "Commercial offer" section |

Building this table first, before drafting prose, is what keeps the response evaluator-focused rather than narrative-focused — every section exists because a criterion demands it, not because it "seems like the kind of thing a proposal should cover."

Value proposition — weak vs. strong

Weak (generic, no evidence): "We are the best provider for this project due to our extensive experience and outstanding value for money."

Strong (specific, evidence-backed): "We have facilitated [N] strategic planning workshops for public-sector clients in the past three years, including [comparable-scale example if disclosable], and our proposed day rates sit [X]% below the panel median for this service category."

The difference is entirely about attaching a specific, checkable claim instead of an unsupported assertion — this is what actually moves an evaluator's score, not confident language.

# Government Tender Response Drafts a response to a government RFQ or RFT (Request for Quote/Tender) — understanding of requirements, value proposition, capability and experience, commercial offer, and standard compliance declarations — following the structure procurement panels actually evaluate against. Workshop Agenda Designer Designs a structured workshop — clear objectives, a timed agenda, participation-driving activities, and facilitation notes — for strategic planning sessions, kickoffs, or stakeholder workshops. Use when the user needs to plan or facilitate a workshop, design a workshop agenda, or run a strategic planning session. ⇩ .zip

Workshop Agenda Designer

When to use this

The user needs to plan a workshop — a session with a group where a specific output (a decision, a plan, an agreed set of priorities) needs to exist by the end, not just a discussion.

When NOT to use this

  • A regular status meeting with no specific workshop-style output needed — a normal agenda is enough.
  • The output/objective genuinely isn't known yet — clarify that first; designing activities before knowing the required output produces a workshop that runs well but doesn't land anywhere.

Workflow

  1. State the required end-output in one sentence before designing anything — "a strategic plan" is vague; "agreed, updated objectives for the next planning cycle, with each objective's KPI defined" is a real target to design toward.
  2. Work backward from that output to the activities that produce it — each agenda item should visibly build toward the stated output, not just be a generally-relevant topic.
  3. Design for participation, not presentation. Long stretches of one person presenting to a passive room produce weak output — build in small-group work, structured discussion prompts, or individual input before group discussion (so quieter voices contribute before the loudest opinion anchors the room).
  4. Allocate realistic time, including breaks. A packed agenda with no breathing room degrades output quality in the back half — budget buffer, not just the optimistic minimum per section.
  5. Write facilitation notes, not just topic titles — what the facilitator actually says to open each section, how to handle a dominant voice or a stalled discussion, and what "done" looks like for each activity before moving on.
  6. Plan output capture explicitly — who's taking notes, in what format, and what artifact exists at the end — a great workshop with no captured output loses most of its value within a week.

Output format

## Workshop: [name]
Required output by end of session: [one sentence]
TimeDurationSectionActivityProduces
...............

### Facilitation notes [Per section: opening prompt, how to handle common friction, what "done" looks like before moving on]

### Output capture [Who captures what, in what format, and what happens to it afterward] ```

Common pitfalls

  • No clearly stated required output — the workshop runs but doesn't land on a decision or artifact.
  • All-presentation, no-participation design.
  • Over-packed agenda with no buffer, degrading the back half of the session.
  • No plan for capturing output — insights discussed live but never written down get lost.

Quality checklist

  • Required output is stated in one sentence before the agenda is built
  • Every agenda item visibly builds toward that output
  • Activities include real participation, not just presentation
  • Time allocation includes buffer/breaks, not just optimistic minimums
  • Output capture method and owner are explicit

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: GIC annual strategic planning workshop

Required output by end of session: agreed priorities for the next 12 months, each with an owner and a rough timeline (feeding into plan-on-a-page).

TimeDurationSectionActivityProduces
9:0015 minOpeningFrame the session's required output; quick round of "one thing going well, one thing you're worried about"Shared context, everyone's spoken once early
9:1545 minCurrent stateIndividually write 3 honest current-state observations, then share and cluster as a groupA shared, non-spin view of where things stand
10:0015 minBreak——
10:1560 minPriority generationSmall-group brainstorm (2 groups of 2) on "what would most move the business forward this year," then cross-shareA long-list of candidate priorities
11:1515 minBreak——
11:3045 minPrioritizationApply impact-effort-prioritizer live to the long-list as a groupA short-list of 3-5 real priorities
12:1530 minOwnership & closeAssign an owner and rough timeline to each priority; confirm next check-in dateThe actual required output

### Facilitation notes - Opening round: keep it to one sentence each — this is about getting everyone's voice in the room early, not a full discussion yet. - Current state section: do the individual-write step before any group discussion — this prevents the most senior or loudest voice from anchoring everyone else's view of "how things are going." - If the brainstorm stalls, prompt with "what's the thing we keep saying we should do but haven't" — usually unlocks real answers.

### Output capture One person (not the facilitator, who should stay focused on running the session) takes notes throughout, using the agenda's "Produces" column as a structure — the final artifact is the priority list with owners, written up and circulated within 2 business days while it's still fresh.

Stakeholder Influence Map Maps stakeholders on an influence-vs-interest grid and recommends an engagement approach for each one. Use when the user needs to identify who needs to be on board for a decision or change, plan how to communicate with different stakeholders, or navigate organizational buy-in. ⇩ .zip

Stakeholder Influence Map

When to use this

A decision, project, or change involves multiple people or groups with different levels of power and interest, and the user needs a plan for how to engage each of them — not just a list of who's involved.

When NOT to use this

  • Only one or two stakeholders are relevant — a simple direct conversation plan is enough, a full grid is overkill.
  • The question is about internal task ownership on a project, not influence/buy-in — use raci-matrix-generator for that instead.

Workflow

  1. List every stakeholder who could affect or be affected by the decision — include people who might resist as well as obvious supporters; an incomplete list is the most common failure mode here.
  2. Rate each on two dimensions: - Influence — how much power they have to help or block this (formal authority, informal sway, control of resources) - Interest — how much this actually matters to them personally or to their goals
  3. Plot into the four quadrants and use the standard engagement approach for each (see references/quadrant-strategies.md for detail): - High influence, high interest → Manage closely (actively involve) - High influence, low interest → Keep satisfied (don't overwhelm, but don't neglect) - Low influence, high interest → Keep informed (they care, give them visibility even without needing their active buy-in) - Low influence, low interest → Monitor (minimal effort, watch for change)
  4. For each stakeholder, note what they likely care about — their actual motivation, not an assumption — since the engagement approach only works if it's aimed at what they actually value.
  5. Flag any stakeholder whose position might change (e.g. someone gaining influence soon, or someone whose interest will spike once the decision becomes visible) — a static map misses this.

Output format

## Stakeholder map: [decision/project]
StakeholderInfluenceInterestQuadrantWhat they likely care aboutEngagement approach
...High/LowHigh/Low.........

### Notes - [Any stakeholder whose position is likely to shift, and when] - [Any stakeholder missing from initial brainstorm that should be double-checked] ```

Common pitfalls

  • An incomplete stakeholder list — missing a quiet-but-high-influence person is the most common and costly mistake here.
  • Guessing at what a stakeholder cares about instead of noting it as an assumption to verify (or actually asking them).
  • Treating the map as static when a reorg, promotion, or upcoming visibility of the decision will change someone's influence or interest.
  • Using the same engagement approach for everyone regardless of quadrant — the point of the grid is that different stakeholders need different handling.

Quality checklist

  • Stakeholder list is genuinely comprehensive, including likely resistors
  • Influence and interest are rated with a stated reason, not just guessed
  • Each stakeholder has a specific engagement approach, not a generic one
  • Any likely change in someone's position is flagged

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Quadrant strategies in detail

## High influence, high interest — Manage closely Actively involve in decisions, not just updates. Give them a genuine voice, seek their input before major moves, and address concerns directly. These are the people who can single-handedly make or break the initiative — under-investing here is the highest-risk mistake on the grid.

## High influence, low interest — Keep satisfied They could block or slow things if annoyed, but don't need or want detailed involvement. Give periodic, high-level updates; avoid over-communicating (which can itself create unwanted interest/scrutiny); make sure they're not surprised by major changes even if they're not actively engaged day to day.

## Low influence, high interest — Keep informed They care and may have useful input or be affected directly, but can't block or significantly accelerate the initiative on their own. Give them real visibility — regular updates, a way to ask questions — since their goodwill matters even without formal power, and disengaging them entirely can create resentment or lost insight.

## Low influence, low interest — Monitor Minimal proactive effort. Include in broad communications but don't invest special engagement time. Re-check periodically in case their influence or interest changes (e.g. a promotion, or the initiative becoming more visible to their area).

Worked example: introducing a new booking system at GIC

  • Owner/decision-maker (you) — High influence, high interest → Manage closely (this is the driving stakeholder, not one being "managed" in the traditional sense, but still worth mapping to keep the plan honest about where the decision authority sits)
  • Booking/inquiry team member — High influence (day-to-day system user, adoption depends on them), high interest (directly affects their daily work) → Manage closely — involve them in choosing/testing the system, not just informing them once decided
  • Boat captains/crew leads — Medium-low influence, medium interest (affects scheduling visibility but not their core job) → Keep informed
  • Existing booking platform/agent partners — Variable influence depending on how much business they route, likely low-medium interest unless the change affects how they submit bookings → Keep satisfied or informed depending on actual integration impact — worth confirming directly rather than assuming

Stakeholder Engagement Log Maintains an ongoing, chronological log of stakeholder engagement — who was contacted, when, why, what was discussed, and what follow-up resulted — as a project runs. Use when the user needs to track engagement history over a project's life, log meeting outcomes with stakeholders, or wants a record of who's been engaged and about what, distinct from a one-time influence/interest map. ⇩ .zip

Stakeholder Engagement Log

When to use this

A project is underway with ongoing stakeholder contact, and the user needs a running record of that contact history — not a one-time prioritization exercise (stakeholder-influence-map covers that separately, and both commonly exist side by side on a real project).

When NOT to use this

  • A one-off engagement with no ongoing relationship to track — a simple note is enough.
  • What's actually needed is the influence/interest prioritization, not a history log — use stakeholder-influence-map.

Workflow

  1. Log each interaction as it happens, or as close to it as practical — reconstructing engagement history from memory weeks later loses accuracy and detail fast.
  2. Capture consistently for every entry: who was involved (both sides), the date, the purpose of the contact, the actual discussion points (not just "had a meeting"), and the outcome or follow-up needed.
  3. Use it operationally, not just as a record — before a new meeting with a stakeholder, check the log for prior context so the conversation doesn't repeat ground already covered.
  4. Review periodically for engagement gaps — a stakeholder who hasn't appeared in the log for a while, especially one who's high-influence, is a risk worth noticing before it becomes a problem.
  5. Use it to brief new team members quickly — a well-kept log lets someone joining the project understand engagement history without a long verbal handover.

Output format

## Stakeholder engagement log: [project]

| # | Stakeholder(s) | Date | Purpose | Discussion points | Outcome / follow-up | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | 1 | ... | ... | ... | ... | ... | ```

Common pitfalls

  • Logging in bulk after the fact instead of as engagement happens — detail and accuracy degrade quickly from memory.
  • Vague entries ("had a catch-up") that give a future reader no useful context.
  • Never reviewing the log for gaps — a stakeholder who's gone quiet is a signal, not a non-event.
  • Confusing this with stakeholder-influence-map — the two serve different purposes (ongoing record vs. one-time prioritization) and a real project benefits from both existing together.

Quality checklist

  • Entries are logged close to when the interaction happened, not reconstructed later
  • Every entry has specific discussion points, not a generic summary
  • Outcomes/follow-ups are captured, not just that a meeting occurred
  • The log is periodically reviewed for stakeholders who've gone quiet

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: GIC Gili route stakeholder engagement log (excerpt)

#Stakeholder(s)DatePurposeDiscussion pointsOutcome / follow-up
1Local dock operator (prospective partner)[date]Initial introduction and feasibility checkDock capacity, mooring depth at low tide, indicative costsScouting trip scheduled to verify low-tide viability directly
2Ops lead (internal)[date]Debrief after scouting tripLow-tide mooring issue confirmed; alternative dock options discussedOps lead to shortlist 2 alternative docks by [date]
3Local dock operator #2 (alternative)[date]Follow-up feasibility checkAvailability, cost, low-tide depth confirmed viableMove forward with this operator pending final agreement

### Using the log operationally Before entry #3's call, the log was checked — it showed exactly why the first operator didn't work out (mooring depth at low tide), so the second conversation could ask that question directly and early, rather than repeating the same discovery process from scratch.

### Reviewing for gaps If a high-influence stakeholder (e.g. the alternative dock operator) hasn't appeared in the log for several weeks after an agreement was expected, that gap itself is worth flagging and following up on — not waiting for them to reach out first.

# Stakeholder Engagement Log Maintains an ongoing, chronological log of stakeholder engagement — who was contacted, when, why, what was discussed, and what follow-up resulted — as a project runs. Use when the user needs to track engagement history over a project's life, log meeting outcomes with stakeholders, or wants a record of who's been engaged and about what, distinct from a one-time influence/interest map. # Stakeholder Engagement Log ## When to use this A project is underway with ongoing stakeholder contact, and the user needs a running record of that contact history — not a one-time prioritization exercise (`stakeholder-influence-map` covers that separately, and both commonly exist side by side on a real project). ## When NOT to use this - A one-off engagement with no ongoing relationship to track — a simple note is enough. - What's actually needed is the influence/interest prioritization, not a history log — use `stakeholder-influence-map`. ## Workflow 1. **Log each interaction as it happens**, or as close to it as practical — reconstructing engagement history from memory weeks later loses accuracy and detail fast. 2. **Capture consistently for every entry**: who was involved (both sides), the date, the purpose of the contact, the actual discussion points (not just "had a meeting"), and the outcome or follow-up needed. 3. **Use it operationally, not just as a record** — before a new meeting with a stakeholder, check the log for prior context so the conversation doesn't repeat ground already covered. 4. **Review periodically for engagement gaps** — a stakeholder who hasn't appeared in the log for a while, especially one who's high-influence, is a risk worth noticing before it becomes a problem. 5. **Use it to brief new team members quickly** — a well-kept log lets someone joining the project understand engagement history without a long verbal handover. ## Output format ``` ## Stakeholder engagement log: [project] | # | Stakeholder(s) | Date | Purpose | Discussion points | Outcome / follow-up | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | 1 | ... | ... | ... | ... | ... | ``` ## Common pitfalls - Logging in bulk after the fact instead of as engagement happens — detail and accuracy degrade quickly from memory. - Vague entries ("had a catch-up") that give a future reader no useful context. - Never reviewing the log for gaps — a stakeholder who's gone quiet is a signal, not a non-event. - Confusing this with `stakeholder-influence-map` — the two serve different purposes (ongoing record vs. one-time prioritization) and a real project benefits from both existing together. ## Quality checklist - [ ] Entries are logged close to when the interaction happened, not reconstructed later - [ ] Every entry has specific discussion points, not a generic summary - [ ] Outcomes/follow-ups are captured, not just that a meeting occurred - [ ] The log is periodically reviewed for stakeholders who've gone quiet # Worked example: GIC Gili route stakeholder engagement log (excerpt) | # | Stakeholder(s) | Date | Purpose | Discussion points | Outcome / follow-up | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | 1 | Local dock operator (prospective partner) | [date] | Initial introduction and feasibility check | Dock capacity, mooring depth at low tide, indicative costs | Scouting trip scheduled to verify low-tide viability directly | | 2 | Ops lead (internal) | [date] | Debrief after scouting trip | Low-tide mooring issue confirmed; alternative dock options discussed | Ops lead to shortlist 2 alternative docks by [date] | | 3 | Local dock operator #2 (alternative) | [date] | Follow-up feasibility check | Availability, cost, low-tide depth confirmed viable | Move forward with this operator pending final agreement | ### Using the log operationally Before entry #3's call, the log was checked — it showed exactly why the first operator didn't work out (mooring depth at low tide), so the second conversation could ask that question directly and early, rather than repeating the same discovery process from scratch. ### Reviewing for gaps If a high-influence stakeholder (e.g. the alternative dock operator) hasn't appeared in the log for several weeks after an agreement was expected, that gap itself is worth flagging and following up on — not waiting for them to reach out first.
Change & program management9 ⇩ download 9
Change Impact Assessment Assesses who and what is impacted by a planned change (people, process, systems, culture) before it's rolled out, so change-management effort targets what actually needs it instead of guessing. Use when the user is planning a system rollout, process change, reorg, or any initiative that changes how people work, and needs to understand impact before building a change plan. ⇩ .zip

Change Impact Assessment (CIA)

When to use this

A change is planned (new system, new process, org restructure) and, before building a rollout or communication plan, the user needs to know specifically who is affected, how much, and in what way.

When NOT to use this

  • The change is trivial/low-impact enough that a full assessment is more process than the decision needs — say so rather than over-formalizing a small tweak.
  • Impact is already well understood and documented — update the existing assessment rather than starting over.

Workflow

  1. Describe the change precisely. "New booking system" is vague; "replacing the shared spreadsheet booking process with a dedicated booking platform, changing how bookings are entered and how availability is checked" is assessable.
  2. Identify every group touched, not just the obvious one — direct users, adjacent teams whose workflow depends on the changed thing, customers/external parties, and anyone whose role changes even if the system/process itself doesn't touch them directly.
  3. For each group, assess impact across dimensions: - Process — what they do differently, step by step - Systems/tools — what they now use instead of / in addition to before - People/roles — whether responsibilities, reporting lines, or required skills change - Culture/ways of working — anything that shifts how the group operates day to day, beyond the mechanical change
  4. Rate the magnitude honestly (e.g. Small / Medium / Large) per group — don't rate everything as "large" for emphasis; a mixed picture is more useful and more credible than uniform severity.
  5. Get this from the people affected, not just from planning assumptions — a quick check with someone actually doing the work catches impacts a top-down view misses.
  6. Feed the result forward — this assessment is normally the input to a change roadmap (change-management-roadmap), a readiness check (change-readiness-assessment), or a communications plan (communication-engagement-plan), not an end in itself.

Output format

## Change impact assessment: [change, precisely described]
GroupProcess impactSystems impactPeople/role impactCulture impactMagnitude
...............Small/Medium/Large

### Groups needing the most support [Who's most affected and why, in plain language]

### Confidence [Where this is based on direct input from affected people vs. assumption] ```

Common pitfalls

  • Guessing impact from a planning document instead of checking with people actually doing the affected work.
  • Rating every group as equally impacted, which tells the reader nothing about where to focus effort.
  • Stopping at the obvious/direct users and missing adjacent teams or external parties who are also affected.
  • Treating this as a one-off exercise when the change design itself changes — re-check if scope shifts.

Quality checklist

  • Every genuinely affected group is included, not just the obvious direct users
  • Each group is rated across process/systems/people/culture, not just one dimension
  • Magnitude ratings are differentiated, not uniformly high
  • Based on input from affected people where possible, with confidence noted where it's assumption instead

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: GIC booking system replacement

Change: replacing the shared spreadsheet booking process with a dedicated booking platform.

GroupProcess impactSystems impactPeople/role impactCulture impactMagnitude
Booking/inquiry staffFull change — availability checked and bookings entered in a new system, not a spreadsheetNew platform to learn; old spreadsheet retiredNone — same role, new toolSome initial frustration expected during learning curveLarge
Ops/crew schedulingMinor — still receives a schedule, just generated from a different sourceViews schedule in new platform instead of shared fileNoneMinimalSmall
AccountingDeposit/payment data now flows from the new platform instead of manual entryNew integration point to understandNoneMinimal, though errors during transition need a fallback planMedium
CustomersBooking confirmation process may look slightly different (new email template, possibly a booking portal)None directlyNoneMinimal — mostly invisible to them if done wellSmall

### Groups needing the most support Booking/inquiry staff — full process and tool change, direct daily impact. This is where training and hands-on support should concentrate; other groups need only light communication.

### Confidence Based on a direct conversation with the two people who currently handle inquiries (high confidence on their impact); accounting and customer impact are based on planning assumptions and should be checked once the platform's integration details are confirmed.

Change Management Roadmap Builds a change management strategy and roadmap using the ADKAR model (Awareness, Desire, Knowledge, Ability, Reinforcement) so a change actually sticks after go-live instead of just being announced. Use when the user needs a change management plan or roadmap for a rollout, system implementation, or organisational change, or explicitly mentions ADKAR. ⇩ .zip

Change Management Roadmap (ADKAR)

When to use this

A change is planned or underway and the user needs a structured plan for how people move from not knowing about it to actually working the new way, sustainably — not just a communications announcement.

When NOT to use this

  • Only a communications plan is needed (who says what, when) without the fuller change-adoption structure — communication-engagement-plan covers that on its own.
  • The change is too small to need structured change management — a one-line heads-up may be all that's needed.

Workflow

  1. Use impact data as the input, ideally from change-impact-assessment — the roadmap should target groups by how much they're actually affected, not treat everyone identically.
  2. Build activities against each ADKAR stage, in order — skipping a stage is the most common reason change doesn't stick: - Awareness — people know the change is coming and why - Desire — people are willing to support and participate in it (this requires addressing "what's in it for me / what am I worried about," not just announcing) - Knowledge — people know how to change (training, documentation) - Ability — people can actually demonstrate the new skill/behavior in practice, not just in a training session - Reinforcement — the change is sustained after go-live (this stage is the one most often skipped, and skipping it is why changes regress to old behavior weeks after launch)
  3. Sequence activities across a timeline — pre-launch (Awareness, Desire, Knowledge), launch (Ability — go-live support), post-launch (Reinforcement — this needs real duration, not a single follow-up email).
  4. Assign an owner to each activity — an unowned change activity doesn't happen.
  5. Plan reinforcement concretely: what specifically happens at 2 weeks, 6 weeks, 3 months post-launch to catch regression to old behavior and address it.

Output format

## Change roadmap: [change]
StageActivityAudienceTimingOwner
Awareness............
Desire............
Knowledge............
Ability............
Reinforcement............

### Reinforcement plan (the stage most often skipped) [Specific checks/support at defined intervals post-launch] ```

Common pitfalls

  • Treating "Awareness" (an announcement) as if it covers the whole change — the other four stages are where adoption actually happens.
  • Designing Knowledge (training) without Ability (practice/support) — people can pass a training session and still fail at applying it live.
  • No Reinforcement plan, or a token one — this is why changes visibly succeed at launch and quietly regress a month later.
  • One-size-fits-all activities for groups with very different impact levels (see change-impact-assessment).

Quality checklist

  • All five ADKAR stages have specific activities, not just Awareness and Knowledge
  • Activities are targeted by group/impact level, not uniform
  • Every activity has an owner
  • Reinforcement has concrete, timed check-ins, not a single post-launch email

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: GIC booking system rollout (booking staff)

StageActivityAudienceTimingOwner
AwarenessTeam meeting explaining why the spreadsheet is being replaced and the rough timelineBooking staff4 weeks before go-liveOwner
Desire1:1 conversation addressing concerns (will this make my job harder before it gets easier?) and what's better about the new wayBooking staff3 weeks beforeOwner
KnowledgeHands-on training session on the new platformBooking staff2 weeks beforePlatform vendor / owner
AbilityShadowed practice period — staff use the new system for real bookings with an experienced person available to helpBooking staffLaunch weekOwner (available on-call)
ReinforcementDaily 10-minute check-in for the first week, then weekly for a month; explicitly ask "what's still going back to the old habit?"Booking staffWeeks 1-4 post-launchOwner

### Reinforcement plan - Day 1-5: daily 10-minute check-in, fix friction immediately - Week 2-4: weekly check-in, watch for reversion to the spreadsheet for "just this one booking" - Week 6: confirm the old spreadsheet is genuinely no longer being used by anyone before considering the change complete

This is the stage a rushed rollout skips — without it, it's common for one or two bookings a week to quietly go back into the old spreadsheet "just this once," and within two months the old habit is fully back.

Change Readiness Assessment Assesses an organisation's or team's readiness for an upcoming change — capability gaps, sentiment, and support needs — based on real evidence rather than assumption, so the change plan addresses actual gaps. Use when the user wants to check readiness before a go-live, needs a training needs analysis, or wants to gauge how prepared people are for a change. ⇩ .zip

Change Readiness Assessment

When to use this

A change is approaching (go-live, rollout, reorg taking effect) and the user wants to know, before it happens, whether the affected group is actually ready — not assume readiness because no one has complained.

When NOT to use this

  • Too early — before the change is concretely defined, there's nothing specific to assess readiness against.
  • As a one-time check with no plan to re-check closer to go-live — readiness can change (for better or worse) as the date approaches.

Workflow

  1. Define readiness dimensions relevant to this change: - Capability — do people have the skills/knowledge needed, or is there a training gap? - Sentiment/attitude — are people supportive, neutral, resistant? (Silence isn't the same as support.) - Communication reach — has the message actually reached everyone who needs it, in a form they've absorbed (not just "was sent")? - Leadership/sponsorship support — do people see their own manager visibly backing the change, not just a general announcement from elsewhere?
  2. Gather real evidence, not assumption — a short survey, a few direct conversations, or manager input. "No one's complained" is not evidence of readiness.
  3. Score readiness per group/dimension, and be specific about what's driving a low score — a low capability score means training is needed; a low sentiment score means the Desire stage (see change-management-roadmap) needs more work, not more training.
  4. Turn gaps into specific actions with owners and a deadline before go-live — an assessment that doesn't lead to action is just a report.
  5. Re-check closer to the go-live date if there's meaningful time between the assessment and launch — readiness isn't static.

Output format

## Readiness assessment: [change], as of [date]
GroupCapabilitySentimentCommunication reachLeadership supportOverall
...Low/Med/HighLow/Med/HighLow/Med/HighLow/Med/High...

### Gaps requiring action before go-live | Gap | Action | Owner | Deadline | |---|---|---|---| | ... | ... | ... | ... |

### Evidence basis [Survey / conversations / manager input — and where this is still assumption] ```

Common pitfalls

  • Treating absence of complaints as evidence of readiness.
  • Vague "medium readiness" scores with no explanation of what's actually missing, making the score useless for planning action.
  • Assessing once, early, and never re-checking as go-live approaches.
  • Skipping sentiment/leadership-support dimensions and only checking capability — a technically-trained but unsupported team still under-adopts.

Quality checklist

  • Scores are based on real evidence (survey/conversations), not assumption — and that basis is stated
  • Each low score has a specific driver, not just a rating
  • Gaps convert into owned, dated actions
  • There's a plan to re-check readiness closer to go-live if time allows

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: readiness check, 1 week before booking system go-live

GroupCapabilitySentimentCommunication reachLeadership supportOverall
Booking staffMedium — trained, but one person missed the sessionMedium — one team member openly worried about slowing down during peak seasonHigh — both aware and have used the test environmentHigh — owner visibly involved in trainingMedium

### Gaps requiring action before go-live | Gap | Action | Owner | Deadline | |---|---|---|---| | One staff member missed training | Schedule a 1:1 catch-up session | Owner | 3 days before go-live | | Concern about slower bookings during peak season | Address directly: agree a temporary "buddy" system for the first two weeks so a second person is available during peak hours | Owner | Before go-live |

### Evidence basis Based on a direct conversation with both booking staff members (high confidence) — sentiment score reflects an actual stated concern, not a guess. Leadership support score is self-assessed by the owner and not independently verified, which is a reasonable limitation for a two-person team.

Communication Engagement Plan Builds a structured communication and engagement plan for a change or program — audience segments, key messages, channels, senders, and timing — so communication is targeted rather than one blanket message to everyone. Use when the user needs a comms plan, engagement roadmap, or is planning how to communicate a change to different audiences. ⇩ .zip

Communication & Engagement Plan

When to use this

A change, program, or initiative needs to be communicated to more than one audience, and a single blanket announcement won't serve everyone's actual information needs.

When NOT to use this

  • One audience, one simple message — just write the message directly.
  • The plan is really about the fuller change-adoption picture (training, reinforcement, not just messaging) — use change-management-roadmap, which this plan feeds into.

Workflow

  1. Segment the audience — ideally from a change-impact-assessment or stakeholder-influence-map — since different groups need different information, not the same message repeated.
  2. For each segment, define what they specifically need to know and by when — a booking-staff audience needs process detail; a customer audience needs only "what changes for you," not internal process detail.
  3. Match channel to audience, not one channel for everyone — a team meeting works for staff, an email works for customers, a notice board works for on-site crew who don't check email often.
  4. Sequence messages across the timeline — don't front-load every detail at first announcement; build understanding progressively (what's changing → why → what it means for you → how to get ready).
  5. Choose the right sender for credibility — some messages land better from a direct manager than from a general company announcement; note who should deliver each message, not just what it says.
  6. Build in two-way engagement, not just broadcast — a channel for questions/feedback, and a plan to actually respond to it. One-way communication that never acknowledges questions erodes trust fast.

Output format

## Communication & engagement plan: [change/program]
AudienceKey messageChannelSenderTiming
...............

### Two-way engagement [How feedback/questions get captured and who responds, and by when]

### Timeline view [Simple sequence: announcement → detail → readiness support → go-live → post-launch] ```

Common pitfalls

  • One message for every audience regardless of what they actually need to know.
  • Front-loading all detail at the first announcement instead of building understanding progressively.
  • No feedback channel, or a feedback channel that's never actually monitored/responded to.
  • Choosing convenience of channel over what the audience actually reads (e.g. email to a crew that doesn't check it regularly).

Quality checklist

  • Messages are tailored per audience, not copy-pasted
  • Channel and sender are chosen for the specific audience, not defaulted
  • Messages are sequenced across time, not delivered all at once
  • A real two-way feedback mechanism exists and has an owner

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: GIC booking system change

AudienceKey messageChannelSenderTiming
Booking staffWhat's changing, why, and what training looks likeTeam meeting + follow-up docOwner4 weeks before
Crew/opsSchedules will come from a new source but nothing changes for how they work day to dayBrief in-person mention at a regular crew meetingOps lead1 week before
CustomersBooking confirmations may look slightly different; nothing they need to do differentlyShort email note alongside their next booking confirmationBooking staff (as part of normal comms)At go-live

### Two-way engagement Booking staff have a direct line to the owner for questions during the transition (not a suggestion box that goes unanswered) — questions get a same-day response during the first two weeks, since that's the period uncertainty is highest.

### Timeline view Announcement (why + timeline) → training detail (what/how) → go-live support (day-of availability) → post-launch check-ins (per the reinforcement plan in change-management-roadmap).

Note customers get almost no advance detail — correctly so, since the change is nearly invisible to them and over-communicating to an unaffected audience creates unnecessary anxiety about a "big change" that isn't big for them.

# Communication Engagement Plan Builds a structured communication and engagement plan for a change or program — audience segments, key messages, channels, senders, and timing — so communication is targeted rather than one blanket message to everyone. Use when the user needs a comms plan, engagement roadmap, or is planning how to communicate a change to different audiences. # Communication & Engagement Plan ## When to use this A change, program, or initiative needs to be communicated to more than one audience, and a single blanket announcement won't serve everyone's actual information needs. ## When NOT to use this - One audience, one simple message — just write the message directly. - The plan is really about the fuller change-adoption picture (training, reinforcement, not just messaging) — use `change-management-roadmap`, which this plan feeds into. ## Workflow 1. **Segment the audience** — ideally from a `change-impact-assessment` or `stakeholder-influence-map` — since different groups need different information, not the same message repeated. 2. **For each segment, define what they specifically need to know and by when** — a booking-staff audience needs process detail; a customer audience needs only "what changes for you," not internal process detail. 3. **Match channel to audience**, not one channel for everyone — a team meeting works for staff, an email works for customers, a notice board works for on-site crew who don't check email often. 4. **Sequence messages across the timeline** — don't front-load every detail at first announcement; build understanding progressively (what's changing → why → what it means for you → how to get ready). 5. **Choose the right sender for credibility** — some messages land better from a direct manager than from a general company announcement; note who should deliver each message, not just what it says. 6. **Build in two-way engagement, not just broadcast** — a channel for questions/feedback, and a plan to actually respond to it. One-way communication that never acknowledges questions erodes trust fast. ## Output format ``` ## Communication & engagement plan: [change/program] | Audience | Key message | Channel | Sender | Timing | |---|---|---|---|---| | ... | ... | ... | ... | ... | ### Two-way engagement [How feedback/questions get captured and who responds, and by when] ### Timeline view [Simple sequence: announcement → detail → readiness support → go-live → post-launch] ``` ## Common pitfalls - One message for every audience regardless of what they actually need to know. - Front-loading all detail at the first announcement instead of building understanding progressively. - No feedback channel, or a feedback channel that's never actually monitored/responded to. - Choosing convenience of channel over what the audience actually reads (e.g. email to a crew that doesn't check it regularly). ## Quality checklist - [ ] Messages are tailored per audience, not copy-pasted - [ ] Channel and sender are chosen for the specific audience, not defaulted - [ ] Messages are sequenced across time, not delivered all at once - [ ] A real two-way feedback mechanism exists and has an owner # Worked example: GIC booking system change | Audience | Key message | Channel | Sender | Timing | |---|---|---|---|---| | Booking staff | What's changing, why, and what training looks like | Team meeting + follow-up doc | Owner | 4 weeks before | | Crew/ops | Schedules will come from a new source but nothing changes for how they work day to day | Brief in-person mention at a regular crew meeting | Ops lead | 1 week before | | Customers | Booking confirmations may look slightly different; nothing they need to do differently | Short email note alongside their next booking confirmation | Booking staff (as part of normal comms) | At go-live | ### Two-way engagement Booking staff have a direct line to the owner for questions during the transition (not a suggestion box that goes unanswered) — questions get a same-day response during the first two weeks, since that's the period uncertainty is highest. ### Timeline view Announcement (why + timeline) → training detail (what/how) → go-live support (day-of availability) → post-launch check-ins (per the reinforcement plan in `change-management-roadmap`). Note customers get almost no advance detail — correctly so, since the change is nearly invisible to them and over-communicating to an unaffected audience creates unnecessary anxiety about a "big change" that isn't big for them.
RAID Log Builder Builds and maintains a RAID log (Risks, Assumptions, Issues, Dependencies) — the standard project-governance artifact tracking what could go wrong, what's being assumed, what's already gone wrong, and what the project depends on. Use when the user needs a RAID log, project governance tracking, or wants to track assumptions and dependencies alongside risks, not just risks alone. ⇩ .zip

RAID Log Builder

When to use this

An ongoing project or program needs a single governance artifact tracking risks, assumptions, issues, and dependencies together — the standard format used for regular project status reporting.

When NOT to use this

  • Only risks need deep treatment (with likelihood/impact scoring and detailed mitigation planning) — use risk-register-builder for that focused exercise; a RAID log is broader but shallower per item, meant for ongoing tracking rather than deep risk analysis.

Workflow

  1. Keep the four categories genuinely distinct — this is the discipline that makes a RAID log useful, not just a list: - Risk — something that *might* happen and would have a negative effect if it did (hasn't happened yet) - Assumption — something believed to be true, not yet verified, that the plan depends on (if wrong, the plan needs to change) - Issue — something that *has already* happened and needs resolving now (not hypothetical anymore) - Dependency — something the project needs from outside itself to proceed (another team, a decision, an external delivery)
  2. Don't let a risk sit in "issue" before it's happened, and don't leave an issue miscategorized as a risk once it's real — reclassify promptly as status changes.
  3. Assign an owner and a status to every item — an item with no owner doesn't get worked.
  4. Review and update on a set cadence (e.g. weekly), not just at creation — a RAID log that isn't maintained becomes actively misleading (it looks current but isn't).
  5. Convert unverified assumptions into confirmed facts or explicit risks as they resolve — an assumption that's been quietly wrong for weeks is a common source of project surprises.

Output format

## RAID log: [project], updated [date]

### Risks | Risk | Likelihood | Impact | Owner | Status | |---|---|---|---|---|

### Assumptions | Assumption | If wrong, impact is... | Owner | Status (unverified/confirmed/false) | |---|---|---|---|

### Issues | Issue | Impact | Owner | Status | Resolution target | |---|---|---|---|---|

### Dependencies | Dependency | Needed from | Needed by (date) | Owner | Status | |---|---|---|---|---| ```

Common pitfalls

  • Blurring risk and issue (something that's already happened still tracked as a "risk").
  • Assumptions written once and never revisited to confirm or invalidate.
  • No owner on an item, so it never actually moves.
  • Treating the log as a one-time deliverable rather than a living document reviewed on a cadence.

Quality checklist

  • Each item is in the correct one of the four categories
  • Every item has an owner and a current status
  • Assumptions are being actively verified, not just logged and forgotten
  • The log has an update date and is reviewed on a real cadence

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: GIC Gili route launch RAID log (excerpt)

### Risks | Risk | Likelihood | Impact | Owner | Status | |---|---|---|---|---| | Weather disrupts scheduled crossings | Medium | Medium | Ops lead | Open |

### Assumptions | Assumption | If wrong, impact is... | Owner | Status | |---|---|---|---| | A local dock partnership can be secured before launch | Launch delayed, or a costlier alternative dock needed | Owner | Unverified | | Fuel costs stay within current budget assumptions | Route pricing/margin needs revisiting | Owner | Unverified |

### Issues | Issue | Impact | Owner | Status | Resolution target | |---|---|---|---|---| | Scouting trip revealed the first-choice dock isn't viable at low tide | Delays confirming route logistics | Ops lead | In progress | 2 weeks |

### Dependencies | Dependency | Needed from | Needed by | Owner | Status | |---|---|---|---|---| | Crew training/scouting completion | Ops team | Before public marketing starts | Ops lead | In progress |

Note the dock issue: it started life as a risk ("dock might not be viable"), and once the scouting trip confirmed the problem, it moved into Issues with a resolution target — this is the reclassification discipline the skill calls out, not a new item created from scratch.

Program Governance Structure Defines the governance structure for a program or project — decision-making forums, escalation paths, meeting cadence, and a decision log — so decisions get made at the right level without every issue escalating to the top. Use when the user is setting up a new program or project and needs a governance model, decision rights, or an escalation structure. ⇩ .zip

Program Governance Structure

When to use this

A program or project of meaningful size is starting, or an existing one is struggling with unclear decision-making (everything escalates, or nothing gets decided), and needs an explicit governance structure.

When NOT to use this

  • A small, single-owner initiative — formal governance structure adds overhead a simple project doesn't need.
  • The ask is really about one specific decision, not an ongoing structure — just make the decision.

Workflow

  1. Map decision types to the right level, roughly: - Operational/day-to-day — the delivery team decides directly, no meeting needed - Scope/budget/timeline changes within tolerance — a working group or steering-level forum - Strategic direction, major budget/scope changes — sponsor or executive-level forum
  2. Define each forum explicitly: purpose, who's in it (and why — membership should be the minimum needed to decide, not everyone interested), cadence, and exactly what decisions it has authority over.
  3. Define an escalation path: what specifically triggers escalation (e.g. "impact beyond X budget or Y week delay"), and to which forum — vague escalation criteria are why everything ends up escalating by default.
  4. Set up a decision log — what was decided, by whom, when, and the rationale — so decisions aren't quietly re-argued weeks later by someone who wasn't in the room.
  5. Right-size the structure — too many forums creates governance fatigue and slows delivery; too few creates a bottleneck at the top. Match the number of forums to the program's actual complexity, not a template.

Output format

## Governance structure: [program]
ForumPurposeMembershipCadenceDecision authority
...............

### Escalation triggers | Trigger | Escalates to | |---|---| | ... | ... |

### Decision log | Date | Decision | Made by | Rationale | |---|---|---|---| ```

Common pitfalls

  • Too many governance layers for the program's actual size, slowing delivery without adding real oversight value.
  • Vague escalation criteria, causing everything (or nothing) to escalate.
  • Membership padded with "interested parties" rather than the minimum needed to actually decide.
  • No decision log, so settled decisions get quietly re-opened later.

Quality checklist

  • Each forum has a clearly stated decision authority, not just a general purpose
  • Escalation triggers are specific and measurable, not vague
  • Number of forums matches the program's actual size/complexity
  • A decision log exists and captures rationale, not just outcomes

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: GIC Gili route launch governance (small-scale, right-sized)

ForumPurposeMembershipCadenceDecision authority
Weekly check-inTrack progress, unblock day-to-day issuesOwner, ops leadWeeklyOperational decisions within existing budget
Go/no-go reviewDecide whether to proceed to public launchOwner (sole decision-maker for a business this size)Once, after scouting + unit economics completeLaunch decision itself

### Escalation triggers | Trigger | Escalates to | |---|---| | Any change requiring budget beyond the originally agreed scouting budget | Owner, immediately (not held for the weekly check-in) | | Any safety-related finding from the scouting trip | Owner, immediately |

### Decision log | Date | Decision | Made by | Rationale | |---|---|---|---| | [date] | Proceed with scouting trip | Owner | Needed before any launch decision can be made responsibly | | [date] | Delay public marketing pending dock resolution | Owner | Scouting trip found the first-choice dock unviable at low tide (see RAID log) |

For a two-person operation, this is deliberately lightweight — one weekly check-in and one go/no-go review, not a multi-layered committee structure a much larger program would need. The discipline that matters here is the escalation triggers and the decision log, not forum count.

The discipline that matters here is the escalation triggers and the decision log, not forum count.
Project Retrospective Runs a structured retrospective on a completed project, program phase, or initiative — what worked, what didn't, root causes, and specific recommendations for next time — so lessons actually change future work instead of being filed away. Use when a project or phase has just finished and the user wants a retrospective, lessons-learned review, or post-mortem (backward-looking; for a forward-looking pre-decision version, see pre-mortem-facilitator). ⇩ .zip

Project Retrospective

When to use this

A project, program phase, or initiative has just wrapped up, and the user wants to capture what actually happened and turn it into improvements for next time — not just a summary of activities.

When NOT to use this

  • The project hasn't started or finished yet — for a pre-decision version of this thinking, use pre-mortem-facilitator instead.
  • The goal is just a status update, not a lessons-learned exercise — use weekly-ops-report or board-investor-memo instead.

Workflow

  1. Gather input broadly, not just from leadership — the people who actually did the work see friction and workarounds that a top-down view misses entirely.
  2. Separate fact from interpretation. "The training session ran two weeks late" is a fact; "the timeline was unrealistic" is an interpretation — capture both, but don't let interpretation pass as fact.
  3. Group findings into what worked, what didn't, and root causes — for a recurring or serious "what didn't work" item, apply root-cause-five-whys rather than stopping at the surface symptom.
  4. Frame findings around the system/process, not blame on individuals — "the approval step had no defined turnaround time" leads to a fixable recommendation; "so-and-so was slow to approve" doesn't, and makes people defensive in future retros.
  5. Turn every real finding into a specific, actionable recommendation — "communicate better" is not actionable; "add a defined 48-hour turnaround expectation to the approval step" is.
  6. Assign an owner to each recommendation. A retrospective with no ownership for its recommendations is a report that gets filed and forgotten — its value is entirely in whether it changes the next project.

Output format

## Retrospective: [project/phase], [dates]

### Context [Brief — what this project/phase was]

### What worked - [Specific, not generic]

### What didn't work (with root cause where it matters) - [Finding] — Root cause: [from 5-Whys or direct evidence]

### Recommendations for next time | Recommendation | Owner | Applies to | |---|---|---| | ... | ... | [next project / ongoing process] | ```

Common pitfalls

  • Only interviewing leadership/sponsors, missing frontline reality.
  • Blame-framed findings that make people defensive rather than useful process fixes.
  • Vague recommendations ("communicate better," "plan more carefully") that no one can actually act on.
  • No ownership assigned, so recommendations never make it into the next project.

Quality checklist

  • Input gathered from people who did the work, not just leadership
  • Findings distinguish fact from interpretation
  • "What didn't work" items include root cause, not just symptom
  • Every recommendation is specific, actionable, and has an owner

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: GIC booking system rollout retrospective

### Context Retrospective on the 6-week booking system replacement project, held 2 weeks after go-live.

### What worked - Hands-on training with shadowed practice (Ability stage) meant staff were comfortable within days, not weeks. - Daily check-ins in week 1 caught small issues before they became habits.

### What didn't work - One staff member missed the initial training session and wasn't caught until the readiness check a week later. Root cause (5-Whys): training was scheduled around one calendar, not checked against both staff members' actual availability — a scheduling process gap, not an individual's fault. - The old spreadsheet was still being used for one edge case (group bookings over 10 people) three weeks post-launch. Root cause: the new platform's group-booking flow wasn't covered in training, since it's used rarely — an actual capability gap, not a habit-reversion issue.

### Recommendations for next time | Recommendation | Owner | Applies to | |---|---|---| | Confirm training attendance against both staff members' calendars before scheduling, not just one | Owner | Next system rollout | | Explicitly cover edge-case workflows (not just the common path) in training, even if rare | Owner | Next system rollout | | Extend the readiness check to 3 weeks post-launch, not 1, to catch low-frequency workflow gaps | Owner | Ongoing process |

Note the group-booking finding: it looked like habit-reversion at first glance, but the root cause was a genuine training gap, not backsliding — worth the extra step of checking before assuming the obvious explanation.

# Project Retrospective Runs a structured retrospective on a completed project, program phase, or initiative — what worked, what didn't, root causes, and specific recommendations for next time — so lessons actually change future work instead of being filed away. Root cause (5-Whys): training was scheduled around one calendar, not checked against both staff members' actual availability — a scheduling process gap, not an individual's fault. - The old spreadsheet was still being used for one edge case (group bookings over 10 people) three weeks post-launch. Root cause: the new platform's group-booking flow wasn't covered in training, since it's used rarely — an actual capability gap, not a habit-reversion issue. ### Recommendations for next time | Recommendation | Owner | Applies to | |---|---|---| | Confirm training attendance against both staff members' calendars before scheduling, not just one | Owner | Next system rollout | | Explicitly cover edge-case workflows (not just the common path) in training, even if rare | Owner | Next system rollout | | Extend the readiness check to 3 weeks post-launch, not 1, to catch low-frequency workflow gaps | Owner | Ongoing process | Note the group-booking finding: it looked like habit-reversion at first glance, but the root cause was a genuine training gap, not backsliding — worth the extra step of checking before assuming the obvious explanation.
Rapid Decision Framework Clarifies decision-making roles using the RAPID framework (Recommend, Agree, Perform, Input, Decide) — who proposes, who must sign off, who executes, who's consulted, and who holds final decision authority. Use when a decision has stalled because ownership is unclear, or to clarify decision rights specifically — distinct from raci-matrix-generator, which covers task/work ownership, not who gets to decide. ⇩ .zip

RAPID Decision Framework

When to use this

A decision (not a task) has unclear ownership — it's stalling, being re-litigated, or nobody's sure who actually gets the final call.

When NOT to use this

  • This is about task/work ownership on a project, not a single decision — use raci-matrix-generator instead. The two frameworks answer different questions and are easy to conflate.

Workflow

  1. Confirm this is genuinely about decision rights, not task execution. "Who does the analysis" is a RACI question; "who gets to decide once the analysis is done" is a RAPID question.
  2. Assign each RAPID role explicitly for this specific decision: - Recommend — who proposes an option and does the supporting analysis - Agree — who must sign off before it proceeds. Keep this list as small as possible — every additional person here is a de facto veto holder - Perform — who executes once the decision is made - Input — who is consulted but doesn't have to be agreed with - Decide — exactly one person or body with the final call. This is the single most important assignment in the whole framework
  3. Watch for the most common failure: too many people holding "Agree." This creates gridlock, since any one of them can block indefinitely — push back on this explicitly if it happens.
  4. Make the assignment visible before the decision process starts, not after it's already stalled — RAPID is most valuable as a preventive structure, not a post-mortem tool.

Output format

## Decision rights: [decision]

| Role | Held by | Note | |---|---|---| | Recommend | ... | | | Agree | ... | keep this list minimal | | Perform | ... | | | Input | ... | | | Decide | ... | exactly one | ```

Common pitfalls

  • Confusing this with RACI — they answer different questions (decision rights vs. task ownership).
  • Too many people holding "Agree," creating gridlock through de facto veto power.
  • No single clear "Decide" holder, leaving the decision bouncing between people indefinitely.

Quality checklist

  • This is confirmed to be about decision rights, not task ownership
  • "Agree" is held by as few people as the decision genuinely requires
  • Exactly one person or body holds "Decide"
  • Roles are assigned before the decision process starts, not after it stalls

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: deciding whether to launch the Gili route

RoleHeld byNote
RecommendOps leadBrings the scouting findings and unit economics
Agree— (none needed)Business is small enough that no separate sign-off layer is needed beyond the decider
PerformOps lead + booking staffExecute the launch once decided
InputBooking staff (customer sentiment from past inquiries)Consulted but doesn't hold sign-off
DecideOwnerSole final call

Note the deliberately empty "Agree" row — for a two-person operation, inventing a sign-off layer just to fill out the framework would add friction with no real governance benefit. RAPID scales down as legitimately as it scales up; the discipline that matters here is still having exactly one clear "Decide" holder, not padding out every role.
