Positioning Pitch Crafter Sharpens a positioning statement or elevator pitch for a business, product, or consulting offering, making clear who it's for, what it does, and why it beats the alternative. Use when the user wants to refine how they describe what they do, needs an elevator pitch, or is working on positioning for a business or offering. ⇩ .zip

Positioning & Pitch Crafter

When to use this

The user needs a clear, sharp way to describe a business, product, or offering — for a pitch, a website, an introduction, or just to clarify their own thinking about what they actually offer and to whom.

When NOT to use this

  • What's being offered isn't actually decided yet — positioning describes something that exists or is concretely planned; if the offering itself is still undefined, that's a different (earlier) conversation.

Workflow

  1. Use the standard positioning statement structure as a forcing function, even if the final pitch doesn't use this exact template: *"For [specific target customer], who [need/problem], [offering] is a [category] that [key benefit]. Unlike [main alternative], we [key differentiator]."*
  2. Make the target customer genuinely specific, not "everyone" or "people who like boats" — a positioning statement that could describe any customer doesn't actually position anything.
  3. Name the real alternative, not a strawman. The alternative to a Lombok boat charter might be a resort stay, a different destination entirely, or a competitor — pick the one actually being chosen against most often, since the differentiator only means something relative to a real alternative.
  4. State the differentiator as a genuine reason to choose this over that alternative — not a generic claim ("quality service") that any competitor could also claim. If the only real differentiator is price, say so plainly rather than dressing it up as something else.
  5. Once the structured statement is solid, adapt it into pitch length — a natural-sounding 15-30 second spoken version loses the template scaffolding but keeps the same core content (who, what, why better).
  6. Check it against a simple test: would a competitor's positioning statement, with the names swapped, sound just as true? If yes, the differentiator isn't sharp enough yet.

Output format

## Positioning: [business/offering]

Structured statement: For [target customer], who [need], [offering] is a [category] that [benefit]. Unlike [alternative], we [differentiator].

Elevator pitch (spoken version, ~20-30 seconds): [natural-sounding paragraph version]

### Differentiation check Would this sound just as true with a competitor's name swapped in? [Yes/No, and if yes, what needs to get sharper] ```

Common pitfalls

  • A target customer so broad it doesn't actually narrow anything ("for travelers").
  • A strawman alternative instead of the real thing being chosen against.
  • A differentiator so generic it could apply to any competitor ("quality and great service").
  • Skipping the swap-test, so a weak differentiator goes unnoticed.

Quality checklist

  • Target customer is genuinely specific
  • The named alternative is the real one, not a strawman
  • The differentiator is a real reason to choose this over that specific alternative
  • Passes the "swap the competitor's name in" test

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: Golden Island Cruises

Weak starting version (illustrative): "We offer amazing boat trips in Lombok with great service and beautiful boats."

Problem: could describe almost any charter operator in the region — fails the swap test immediately.

Sharpened version

Structured statement: For travelers who want an authentic, uncrowded island experience without giving up comfort, Golden Island Cruises is a phinisi boat charter operator that combines traditional Indonesian craftsmanship with a genuinely experienced local crew. Unlike large-group day-trip operators, we run smaller, owner-operated charters where the crew's local knowledge shapes the actual route, not just a fixed itinerary.

Elevator pitch (spoken): "We run phinisi boat charters out of Lombok — traditional wooden boats, but with real comfort. What makes us different from the big day-trip operators is that we're small and owner-run, so the crew's actual local knowledge shapes where you go, instead of everyone getting the same fixed stops."

### Differentiation check Would a large day-trip operator's positioning sound just as true with "Golden Island Cruises" swapped in? No — "large-group," "fixed itinerary," and "owner-operated, crew-shaped route" are specific claims that wouldn't fit a big-fleet operator's actual model. That's what makes this differentiator real rather than generic marketing language.

Note what changed from the weak version: "great service" (unfalsifiable, generic) became "owner-operated, crew's local knowledge shapes the route" (specific, checkable, genuinely differentiating from a named alternative type of competitor).

# Positioning Pitch Crafter Sharpens a positioning statement or elevator pitch for a business, product, or consulting offering, making clear who it's for, what it does, and why it beats the alternative. Use when the user wants to refine how they describe what they do, needs an elevator pitch, or is working on positioning for a business or offering. # Positioning & Pitch Crafter ## When to use this The user needs a clear, sharp way to describe a business, product, or offering — for a pitch, a website, an introduction, or just to clarify their own thinking about what they actually offer and to whom. ## When NOT to use this - What's being offered isn't actually decided yet — positioning describes something that exists or is concretely planned; if the offering itself is still undefined, that's a different (earlier) conversation. ## Workflow 1. **Use the standard positioning statement structure** as a forcing function, even if the final pitch doesn't use this exact template: *"For [specific target customer], who [need/problem], [offering] is a [category] that [key benefit]. Unlike [main alternative], we [key differentiator]."* 2. **Make the target customer genuinely specific**, not "everyone" or "people who like boats" — a positioning statement that could describe any customer doesn't actually position anything. 3. **Name the real alternative**, not a strawman. The alternative to a Lombok boat charter might be a resort stay, a different destination entirely, or a competitor — pick the one actually being chosen against most often, since the differentiator only means something relative to a real alternative. 4. **State the differentiator as a genuine reason to choose this over that alternative** — not a generic claim ("quality service") that any competitor could also claim. If the only real differentiator is price, say so plainly rather than dressing it up as something else. 5. **Once the structured statement is solid, adapt it into pitch length** — a natural-sounding 15-30 second spoken version loses the template scaffolding but keeps the same core content (who, what, why better). 6. **Check it against a simple test**: would a competitor's positioning statement, with the names swapped, sound just as true? If yes, the differentiator isn't sharp enough yet. ## Output format ``` ## Positioning: [business/offering] **Structured statement**: For [target customer], who [need], [offering] is a [category] that [benefit]. Unlike [alternative], we [differentiator]. **Elevator pitch (spoken version, ~20-30 seconds)**: [natural-sounding paragraph version] ### Differentiation check Would this sound just as true with a competitor's name swapped in? [Yes/No, and if yes, what needs to get sharper] ``` ## Common pitfalls - A target customer so broad it doesn't actually narrow anything ("for travelers"). - A strawman alternative instead of the real thing being chosen against. - A differentiator so generic it could apply to any competitor ("quality and great service"). - Skipping the swap-test, so a weak differentiator goes unnoticed. ## Quality checklist - [ ] Target customer is genuinely specific - [ ] The named alternative is the real one, not a strawman - [ ] The differentiator is a real reason to choose this over that specific alternative - [ ] Passes the "swap the competitor's name in" test # Worked example: Golden Island Cruises Weak starting version (illustrative): "We offer amazing boat trips in Lombok with great service and beautiful boats." Problem: could describe almost any charter operator in the region — fails the swap test immediately. ### Sharpened version **Structured statement**: For travelers who want an authentic, uncrowded island experience without giving up comfort, Golden Island Cruises is a phinisi boat charter operator that combines traditional Indonesian craftsmanship with a genuinely experienced local crew. Unlike large-group day-trip operators, we run smaller, owner-operated charters where the crew's local knowledge shapes the actual route, not just a fixed itinerary. **Elevator pitch (spoken)**: "We run phinisi boat charters out of Lombok — traditional wooden boats, but with real comfort. What makes us different from the big day-trip operators is that we're small and owner-run, so the crew's actual local knowledge shapes where you go, instead of everyone getting the same fixed stops." ### Differentiation check Would a large day-trip operator's positioning sound just as true with "Golden Island Cruises" swapped in? No — "large-group," "fixed itinerary," and "owner-operated, crew-shaped route" are specific claims that wouldn't fit a big-fleet operator's actual model. That's what makes this differentiator real rather than generic marketing language. Note what changed from the weak version: "great service" (unfalsifiable, generic) became "owner-operated, crew's local knowledge shapes the route" (specific, checkable, genuinely differentiating from a named alternative type of competitor).
Negotiation Prep Structures preparation for a negotiation — each side's underlying interests (not just stated positions), BATNA and walk-away points, and possible multi-issue trades — using an interest-based negotiation approach. Use when the user is preparing for a negotiation (contract, deal, supplier terms, salary) or mentions BATNA or a walk-away point. ⇩ .zip

Negotiation Prep

When to use this

An actual negotiation is coming up — a contract, supplier terms, a deal, a rate — and the user wants to walk in prepared rather than reactive.

When NOT to use this

  • A trivial, low-stakes exchange where preparation effort isn't worth it — just have the conversation.

Workflow

  1. Separate positions from interests, for both sides. A position is what someone asks for ("$50 a unit"); the interest is why they want it (cash-flow timing, margin protection, precedent for other deals). Interests are usually more flexible and more creative to solve for than the stated position.
  2. Define your BATNA (Best Alternative To a Negotiated Agreement) — what happens if this negotiation fails and you walk away. This is what actually sets your real power in the room, not how reasonable your ask feels.
  3. Estimate the other side's likely BATNA too, even roughly — it tells you how much flexibility they actually have, independent of how firm they sound.
  4. Set a target and a genuine walk-away point — the range you'd be happy with, and the point below (or above) which you actually leave the table. Decide this before the room, not during it.
  5. Look for multi-issue trades, not just the one obvious number. Two parties fixated on a single price figure often miss value that's easy to unlock once timing, volume, term length, or other terms are brought into the mix.
  6. Plan your opening move and responses to common tactics — anchoring, long silences, artificial deadlines — so they don't catch you improvising in the moment.

Output format

## Negotiation prep: [what/who]
Your sideTheir side
Stated position......
Underlying interests......
BATNA...... (estimate)
Target / walk-away range...n/a

### Possible multi-issue trades [Beyond the headline number — timing, volume, term, other terms]

### Opening move & tactic responses [Your first move, and how you'll handle anchoring/silence/deadline pressure] ```

Common pitfalls

  • Negotiating only on stated positions, never surfacing interests — leaves value on the table that both sides could have captured.
  • No real BATNA, or an unrealistic one — makes any deal look better than it is, weakening your actual position.
  • Fixating on a single issue instead of looking for trades across multiple terms.

Quality checklist

  • Interests are identified separately from stated positions, both sides
  • BATNA is realistic, not aspirational
  • A genuine walk-away point is set before the negotiation, not during it
  • At least one multi-issue trade is considered beyond the headline number

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: GIC renegotiating fuel supplier terms

GIC (buyer)Fuel supplier
Stated positionWants a lower per-litre rateWants to hold current pricing
Underlying interestsPredictable cost for margin planning; doesn't necessarily need the lowest possible headline rateWants volume certainty and reliable payment timing more than the exact rate
BATNACould switch to Supplier B at a slightly higher rate but with less reliable deliveryCould lose the account, but GIC is a meaningful share of their local volume
Target / walk-away rangeTarget: 5% reduction. Walk-away: no worse than current rate with better payment termsn/a

### Possible multi-issue trades Since the supplier's real interest is volume certainty and payment timing more than the exact per-litre number, a 12-month volume commitment in exchange for a smaller rate reduction plus better payment terms (e.g. 30-day instead of 14-day) may satisfy both sides better than purely fighting over the headline rate.

### Opening move & tactic responses Open by naming the volume-commitment trade directly rather than just asking for a discount — reframes the conversation around a trade instead of a one-sided ask. If met with an anchored "we can't move on price," respond by moving the conversation to payment terms rather than repeating the price ask.

Capability Competency Matrix Builds a role-based competency or development framework — the skills expected at each seniority level for a team's roles — used for hiring bars, performance conversations, and staff development planning. Use when the user needs a skills matrix, competency framework, or development framework for staff or team roles. ⇩ .zip

Capability / Competency Matrix

When to use this

The user manages or is designing a team structure and needs a shared, concrete definition of what's expected at each seniority level — for hiring, performance conversations, or development planning.

When NOT to use this

  • A single, one-off hiring decision with no need for an ongoing framework — a simple job description is enough.
  • The framework would immediately go unused (see pitfalls) — worth confirming there's a real plan to use it before building it.

Workflow

  1. List the disciplines/skill areas relevant to what this team actually does — generic categories borrowed from elsewhere are less useful than ones specific to the real work (e.g. for a consulting team: client management, problem-solving/analysis, change management, project management, business development — adapt to the actual team).
  2. Define the seniority levels relevant to this team (e.g. Consultant → Senior Consultant → Manager → Senior Manager → Director, or whatever titles/levels actually exist).
  3. For every discipline × level cell, write a real description of what "good" looks like at that level — not just a 1-5 rating with no definition. "Has led a project applying this discipline and holds relevant accreditation" is usable in a real conversation; a bare number 4/5 is not.
  4. Distinguish level progression clearly — what specifically changes between "foundational" and "strong" for a given discipline, so the matrix can actually answer "what does this person need to do to get to the next level."
  5. State explicitly how the framework will be used (hiring bar, development conversations, promotion criteria) — a framework built and never referenced in an actual conversation has no value; note this so it doesn't become a shelf document.

Output format

## Competency framework: [team/roles]

Legend: [level definitions, e.g. Basic / Foundational / Strong / Expert]

Discipline[Level 1][Level 2][Level 3][Level 4]
...[description][description][description][description]

### How this gets used [Hiring / development conversations / promotion criteria — specific, not generic] ```

Common pitfalls

  • Rating cells with a number and no description, which is unusable in an actual coaching conversation.
  • Disciplines too generic to help a manager give specific feedback.
  • Building the framework and never actually referencing it in hiring or development conversations — the most common way this becomes wasted effort.
  • Levels that don't clearly distinguish from each other, making progression criteria fuzzy.

Quality checklist

  • Disciplines are specific to what this team actually does
  • Every cell has a real description, not just a rating
  • Level-to-level progression is clear enough to guide a development conversation
  • There's a stated, real use for the framework (not a shelf document)

Deeper methodology & worked examples

Worked example: illustrative consulting-team competency framework

Legend: Basic (negligible/basic exposure) / Foundational (led a workstream) / Strong (led projects, holds relevant accreditation) / Expert (leads numerous significant projects, recognized authority)

DisciplineBasicFoundationalStrongExpert
Client managementAttends client meetings, minimal independent contactManages day-to-day client relationship on one workstreamManages the overall client relationship on a project independentlyTrusted advisor across multiple client relationships, brought in for the hardest conversations
Business analysis / problem-solvingSupports analysis under close directionHas independently structured and analyzed a defined problemLeads problem structuring on ambiguous, high-stakes questionsSets the analytical approach across a program, coaches others in it
Change managementBasic understanding, no independent deliveryDelivered change activities (e.g. training sessions) under a change leadHas led a change workstream end to end, holds relevant accreditationDesigns change strategy for large, complex, multi-release programs

### How this gets used - Hiring: candidates are assessed against the Foundational bar for the discipline most central to the role they're hiring for, not every discipline equally. - Development conversations: quarterly 1:1s reference specific cells — "you're Foundational on change management; the gap to Strong is leading a workstream end to end, not just delivering activities under someone else's lead" — concrete enough to set a real development goal against. - Promotion criteria: progression to the next title requires Strong in at least two disciplines, not just tenure — makes the promotion bar explicit rather than a judgment call with no shared reference point.

# Capability Competency Matrix Builds a role-based competency or development framework — the skills expected at each seniority level for a team's roles — used for hiring bars, performance conversations, and staff development planning. **Hiring**: candidates are assessed against the Foundational bar for the discipline most central to the role they're hiring for, not every discipline equally. - **Development conversations**: quarterly 1:1s reference specific cells — "you're Foundational on change management; the gap to Strong is leading a workstream end to end, not just delivering activities under someone else's lead" — concrete enough to set a real development goal against. - **Promotion criteria**: progression to the next title requires Strong in at least two disciplines, not just tenure — makes the promotion bar explicit rather than a judgment call with no shared reference point.

Built for a management-consulting & small-business workflow. Each entry here is a real SKILL.md — open a category, expand a skill, and you're reading the exact instructions Claude follows when it triggers.

⇩ .zip

1 / 8

What is a skill, really?

Think of a skill as a template you hand to an AI ahead of time — a plain text file, no programming involved, that says "when someone asks about X, use this framework, in this order." You don't have to learn any special syntax to use one; you just ask your normal question, and the AI follows the template.

2 / 8

The easy way: copy and paste

This is the fastest way to use any skill here, and it needs zero setup — no account settings, no uploads, works on your phone.

1. Open any skill on this page and click the Copy button.

2. Start a new chat — Claude, ChatGPT, Gemini, whatever you already use.

3. Paste it in, add your real question right underneath it, and send.

[pasted skill]

Why did our bookings drop last month?

That's the whole thing. You'll need to paste it again next time you open a new chat — nothing is saved automatically with this method — but there's nothing to install or break, either.

3 / 8

How do you know it actually worked?

An AI using a framework doesn't always announce it, so here's a reliable way to check: if the reply follows the shape of what you pasted — the same headings, the same steps in the same order — it worked.

If you're ever unsure, or want to make sure a specific framework gets used, just say its name directly in your message. No special syntax — this always works, on any of the methods in this guide:

"Using the SWOT strategic review approach above, look at our three biggest competitors"

4 / 8

Want it to happen automatically? (optional)

Pasting a skill in each time is completely fine for occasional use. If you find yourself reaching for the same one often, Claude specifically can remember it permanently, so you never paste it again — it just quietly reaches for the right one whenever it's relevant, the same way it would for anything already built into it.

This is called "installing" a skill. It's genuinely optional — it doesn't unlock anything the copy-paste method above can't already do, it just saves you a step later. The next two cards cover how, depending on which version of Claude you use.

5 / 8

Installing on claude.ai or the Claude Desktop app

These two work identically — same account, same steps, no Terminal involved anywhere here.

1. Turn on Settings → Capabilities → Code execution — a one-time toggle; skills won't appear as an option until it's on.

2. Download a skill's zip from this page (the "⇩ .zip" link), then go to Settings → Customize → Skills → Upload and select it.

3. Find it in your skills list and switch it on — this is the single easiest thing to miss. New uploads start switched off, so if nothing seems to change afterward, check this first.

6 / 8

Installing on Claude Code (only if you use it)

Claude Code is a different tool from claude.ai/Desktop — it runs in a Terminal window or a code editor. If that sentence doesn't mean anything to you, you're not using it, and card 5 is the one you want instead.

Claude Code checks one specific folder for skills: ~/.claude/skills/ (it created this folder automatically the first time you used it). Easiest way in: download a skill's zip, double-click to unzip it, then drag the unzipped folder into that folder in Finder (press Cmd + Shift + G, type ~/.claude/skills/, Enter).

Prefer Terminal? This one line installs all 50 at once:

cp -r claude-skills-library/skills/* ~/.claude/skills/

Terminal commands that succeed often print nothing, which feels unsettling the first time. To see proof, run:

ls ~/.claude/skills/

You should see a list of skill names print out — that's your confirmation.

7 / 8

Does this work with ChatGPT, Gemini, or other AI tools?

Yes, for the copy-paste method (card 2). A skill is just plain text instructions — any AI chatbot can follow text you paste in, not only Claude. That's true whether you're on ChatGPT, Gemini, or anything else.

The "install it once, never paste again" upgrade (cards 4–6) is Claude-specific — that part relies on a feature built into Claude itself. ChatGPT has a rough equivalent ("Custom GPTs") and Gemini has "Gems," where you can add a skill's text as permanent instructions for a custom assistant — the setup screens look different, but the same skill content works there too, pasted in as its instructions once.

8 / 8

If something feels off

Copy button didn't do anything? Some browsers block copying without a secure connection — a small popup appears instead with the text already selected; just copy it manually (Cmd/Ctrl + C).

Wrong framework used, or none at all? Name it directly in your message (card 3) — that always works, regardless of why the automatic match didn't fire.

Installed via claude.ai/Desktop but nothing changed? Double-check it's switched on — new uploads default to off (card 5).

Installed via Claude Code but nothing changed? Re-run the copy command from card 6 — a downloaded zip on its own isn't enough, it has to actually be copied into ~/.claude/skills/.
